# Full removal and replacement ledger

Every inherited edit includes exact removed and replacement text. Relocations are not substantive deletions.

## 1. developmental_replacement

Source: docs/reading-edition/opening.tex. Destination: docs/developmental-rewrite/2026-10-29/retained/reading-edition/opening.tex.

Reconstruct the argument and reading route.

Selection, intervention alternatives, four journeys, expert comparator, mathematical depth, evidence distinctions and cutoff retained; all details remain reachable.

Before:

~~~text
\chapter*{The decision before the model}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{The decision before the model}

A bank considering a higher deposit rate faces a deceptively simple question:
will the extra funding be worth the interest it must pay? Customers who choose
a generous rate may already intend to move money. An increase in recorded
balances may be a transfer from another account at the same bank. Competitors
may change their prices at the same time. A forecast of balances, however
accurate, cannot by itself separate these explanations.

Causal analysis asks how the bank's choice changes an outcome relative to a
specified alternative. The alternative matters as much as the proposed action.
An invitation to open an account, activation of that account, and a change in
the rate paid on an existing balance are different interventions. Their costs,
beneficiaries and effects need not coincide. A useful analysis begins with this
economic distinction and carries it through the data, statistical assumptions
and financial calculation.

The same discipline applies beyond deposits. A collections team choosing
when to contact a borrower must account for information revealed by earlier
contacts. A lender changing an eligibility threshold must distinguish the
effect on marginal applicants from the effect on all customers. Treasury must
separate an external funding shock from the bank's own response. In each case,
the bank wants to compare feasible decisions, and the available observations
may support only part of that comparison.

The argument develops from these choices. Economic questions lead to causal
models; the models reveal which comparisons the data can identify; estimation
then measures those comparisons and their uncertainty. Repeated decisions and
imperfect observations complicate the analysis. Existing software offers many
of the necessary methods, but its assumptions and definitions must match the
question. Language models may help economists formulate questions and examine
evidence. Public data and controlled numerical studies can establish which
ideas merit a prototype before a bank pays for a larger experiment.

The proposal is for a common analytical service that makes this work easier to
repeat without changing the underlying economics. Its case rests on the
quality and cost of completed studies, including studies that show why an
effect cannot be learned from the available information. The reference
alternative is a capable quantitative team using established methods. A new
service must justify its additional cost against that alternative.

The mathematical development remains part of the argument. Symbols identify
economic quantities, assumptions state what the comparison requires, and
examples expose where a conclusion fails. Constructed examples and simulated
populations are described as such. Findings from public data retain their
population and measurement limits. Proposed capabilities are distinguished
from the calculations that have actually been performed. The literature
cutoff is 7 October 2026; source editions and acquisition dates are documented
separately.

\chapter*{The argument in nine parts}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{The argument in nine parts}

Part I starts with a bank's decision and develops the distinction between
selection, identification and estimation. Part II follows customers and
institutions through time. Part III considers uncertain causal relationships,
imperfect records and the transfer of results between populations.

Parts IV and V compare available methods and software. Discovery and
identification come first, followed by effect estimation, repeated decisions,
panel comparisons and commercial choices. Detailed package discussions sit
beside the questions they address, so that a reader can compare alternatives
without following the order in which they were acquired.

Part VI examines assistance from language models: turning a business question
into a research design, drawing and questioning causal diagrams, supporting
discovery, and explaining results. Part VII develops practical use through
research libraries, command-line tools and the Model Context Protocol. These
implementation choices follow the economic and statistical requirements.

Part VIII brings public data and banking studies together. It asks what the
evidence establishes, how the conclusions depend on information and assumptions,
and where another measurement would be more useful than another estimator.
Part IX turns those findings into an investment question: what to build, what
to reuse, and which experiment could change the choice.

~~~

After:

~~~text
\chapter*{What the bank should buy first}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{What the bank should buy first}

A bank considering a better deposit rate must pay for money it already holds
as well as money the offer attracts. A collections team must account for how
today's contact changes tomorrow's information. A discovery algorithm may
return several explanations of the same observations, with opposite implications
for a pricing decision. An apparently clean customer table may still conceal
misrecorded treatment or incorrectly linked accounts. These are four different
reasons why a persuasive numerical answer can fail to improve a decision.

An increase in one account's recorded balance can be a transfer from another
account at the same bank. The funding outcome must aggregate the intended
household and distinguish new external money from internal movement. Competitor
repricing can also change balances during the same period. A lender's threshold
comparison concerns the applicants affected at that margin, while a treasury
shock analysis must separate the external shock from the bank's own response.
These economic distinctions determine which comparison is useful before a
statistical model is chosen.

The proposal is to make a small class of defensible studies easier to conduct
and compare. The first investment should be a comparative integration using
established methods, a precise study description and a report that preserves
uncertainty. A capable economist using existing tools is the reference
alternative. The proposed service earns a place only if the completed study
is better or cheaper at equivalent quality. Broad replacement of the bank's
statistical estate is not justified by the evidence presented here.

That conclusion follows from the examples, rather than from an assumed
advantage of a common interface. In the constructed offer study, a population
balance effect is carried through sampling uncertainty into annual contribution.
The servicing calculation shows why a correctly fitted regression can still
answer the wrong policy question. The graph example gives identical observed
laws with different intervention values. The observation example shows how
additional measurement knowledge can reverse a financial conclusion. Each
case tells us what a service must preserve and what it must decline to infer.

The evidence has different roles. Established causal results justify inference
under stated assumptions. The project's exact derivations and generated studies
expose target errors, weak support and unresolved models. Public data reveal
realistic timing and measurement difficulties. Local interface executions
establish that particular requests retain their meaning through particular
clients. None of these substitutes for an independently reviewed study on
authentic bank information. Their combined value is to identify a useful and
bounded next experiment before undertaking that more expensive work.

\chapter*{How to read the argument}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{How to read the argument}

The main argument starts with the annual deposit decision, then completes four
worked banking journeys. The foundational chapters develop the identification,
learning, longitudinal and observation arguments on which those journeys rely;
their derivations remain part of the book. The ensuing synthesis brings the
executed studies, seventeen software areas, language assistance, practical use
and investment account into one decision. It ends by specifying what evidence
would reverse the recommendation.

The remaining parts provide the detailed comparative and operating treatments.
They retain alternative estimands, proofs, source-specific restrictions,
negative findings and implementation evidence. The software synthesis points
to the questions that make those comparisons consequential. The operating
treatments explain how the proposed study semantics survive storage, recovery
and client boundaries; they support implementation work after the reader has
seen the economist's complete study. The final economic material develops
additional study designs and the value of information. Detailed sections remain
available through the PDF bookmarks.

Throughout the opening case, assignment of an offer, delivery of that offer,
acceptance and the resulting price are separate quantities. Balances and
contribution have different symbols and a stated one-year horizon. Other
journeys introduce their own units and decision dates explicitly. A number
from a constructed example is not a bank forecast. A method documented in an
inspected source is not thereby an executed integration. These distinctions
are essential to interpreting the proposal.

Research coverage retains the cutoff of 7 October 2026. Editions, acquisition
dates, inspected anchors and omissions remain in the source records. The
comparative recommendation does not claim that every literature or language
cell is complete. The unanswered questions that could change the first
investment are stated where that choice is made.

~~~

## 2. target_correction

Source: docs/reading-edition/banking-decision.tex. Destination: docs/developmental-rewrite/2026-10-29/retained/reading-edition/banking-decision.tex.

Unify annual horizon and distinguish contribution from balances.

Household weighting and selection argument retained; the quarterly illustration is explicitly superseded by the annual target. No observed quarterly result existed.

Before:

~~~text
$Y_i(1)$ be the contribution over the next quarter if the offer is sent and
$Y_i(0)$ the contribution under the alternative. Contribution includes the
agreed funding value, interest expense and servicing costs over the same
quarter. The average effect for the eligible population is
\[
 \tau=\mathbb E\{Y_i(1)-Y_i(0)\}.
\]
~~~

After:

~~~text
$C_i(1)$ be contribution over the following year if the offer is assigned and
$C_i(0)$ contribution over that same year under the alternative. Contribution
includes the agreed funding value, interest expense and servicing costs.
Assignment, delivery, acceptance and the resulting price are distinguished
in Chapter~\ref{rewrite:deposit}. The average assignment effect is
\[
 \tau_C=\mathbb E\{C_i(1)-C_i(0)\}.
\]
~~~

## 3. assumption_clarification

Source: docs/reading-edition/banking-decision.tex. Destination: docs/developmental-rewrite/2026-10-29/retained/reading-edition/banking-decision.tex.

Specify the price rule behind the unchanged expense equation.

Annual accounting, all amounts, omissions and break-even figure retained; voluntary activation requires another cost map.

Before:

~~~text
Suppose
a rate increase attracts an additional balance $\Delta B$ for one year.
~~~

After:

~~~text
For the price rule used here, assignment delivers the offer and reprices
the eligible balance automatically. Suppose the resulting rate increase
attracts an additional average balance $\Delta B$ for one year.
~~~

## 4. target_correction

Source: docs/reading-edition/banking-decision.tex. Destination: docs/developmental-rewrite/2026-10-29/retained/reading-edition/banking-decision.tex.

Separate acceptance from receipt in the selection example.

Selected group composition, 80-versus-20 contrast and selection qualification retained.

Before:

~~~text
Comparing customers who accepted an offer with those who did not generally
mixes the offer's effect with the reasons for acceptance.
~~~

After:

~~~text
Comparing customers who received an offer with those who did not can
mix its effect with the reasons for targeted delivery. Comparing acceptors
with nonacceptors introduces a separate selection problem. The constructed
comparison below concerns receipt, denoted $A$, and balances, denoted $B$.
~~~

\part{From evidence to a bounded investment}
\label{rewrite:part-synthesis}
The banking journeys and their foundations determine what a useful service
must preserve. The next chapters connect software choices, assistance,
observed studies, practical capabilities and the full cost account. Their
conclusion selects the first experiment. Detailed comparative and operating
treatments remain in the following parts.
